TWO TIERS. Each lane leads with its top 12 stories in full - headline, outlet, timestamp, summary, link - and then ends with an 'ALSO IN <LANE>' list of further stories as bare headlines. Demoted is not rejected: those cleared the same date filter and merely ranked lower on cross-outlet pickup, which is a weak signal. Read them. If one matters, use it - but you have only the headline, so say only what the headline supports and invent nothing to pad it out. Full-text items are never demoted.

LANE NAMES DESCRIBE THE FEED, NOT THE TOPIC. SCMP sits in East Asia but carries US politics; Al Jazeera sits in Middle East but carries US domestic stories. Never infer a story's subject from the lane it appears under.

One of the most important outcomes of the recent summit in Washington between U.S. President Donald Trump and his Chinese counterpart, Xi Jinping, is something we are unlikely to learn soon, if ever. This has nothing to do with the failure of the delegations to release a joint statement, or even the large discrepancies between each side’s readout of their meetings. Rather, it is about the private estimates that Xi must have made at the conclusion of his visit concerning two related questions: Who, deep down, is Trump, and what does his presidency say about the state of the United States as the world’s incumbent superpower? One of the most important outcomes of the recent summit in Washington between U.S. President Donald Trump and his Chinese counterpart, Xi Jinping, is something we are unlikely to learn soon, if ever. This has nothing to do with the failure of the delegations to release a joint statement, or even the large discrepancies between each side’s readout of their meetings. Rather, it is about the private estimates that Xi must have made at the conclusion of his visit concerning two related questions: Who, deep down, is Trump, and what does his presidency say about the state of the United States as the world’s incumbent superpower? A column like this is necessarily speculative, so I am going to venture my best guesses. The first is by far the easiest: Trump’s extraordinary unctuousness in giving Xi the red-carpet treatment and flattering him at every turn stands no chance of turning the two men into genuine friends, as Trump often seems to believe. Like any nation, China has made many errors and miscalculations over the decades, but in the post-Mao era, it generates policies through a disciplined process, whose goal is a sober and rational assessment of China’s—or at least the Chinese Communist Party’s (CCP’s)—interests. Friendship in the normal, personalized sense of the word has little to do with this. This is a rule that I would even extend to the theatrics of bonhomie played out between Xi and Russian President Vladimir Putin, whose Moscow serves as Beijing’s situational ally. An admittedly more conjectural thought is that Xi may have found it highly amusing to watch Trump perform his interpretation of what authoritarian leadership consists of. Welcome back to World Brief, where we’re looking at U.S.-Chinese trade negotiations, a failed suspected terrorist attack on a British air base, and Spain ’s worsening housing crisis. China and the United States have agreed to slash tariffs on a slew of “nonsensitive” products collectively worth $60 billion, from Chinese toys to U.S. cosmetics. The cuts represent a step toward reducing the rival superpowers’ trade war after sweeping U.S. duties on Chinese goods reached a whopping 145 percent last year. According to U.S. Trade Representative Jamieson Greer, the tariff cuts will unlock “improved market access” for roughly 30 percent of U.S. exports to China, including agricultural goods such as corn, wheat, and dairy. Notably absent from that list, though, is soybeans. In 2025, China was the top market for U.S. soybean farmers, and soybeans were the agricultural sector’s No. 2 bulk export in value. But with Beijing punishing the U.S. soybean sector in retaliation for President Donald Trump’s trade war, a key Republican voting base has struggled to recover from the heavy economic losses. U.S. and Chinese officials have at least three more months to come up with a solution on soybeans and other unresolved trade issues, with China’s Commerce Ministry confirming on Monday that the countries’ tariff truce will extend through Jan. 10. Yet it is unclear whether the negotiators will have better luck clinching a deal than Trump and Chinese President Xi Jinping, who met in the Oval Office on Thursday to discuss ways to improve the bilateral relationship. Although both leaders praised the importance of a good partnership, the summit resulted in few substantial breakthroughs . Instead, dialogue appeared to get bogged down by disagreements over Taiwan , with Xi urging Trump to oppose the island’s independence. For most of the past year, Slovenia’s official web domain registrar has recorded just around a thousand new registrations a month. That’s to be expected—after all, the small Eastern European country is home to only 2.1 million people. But last week, after U.S. President Donald Trump addressed the United Nations General Assembly in New York, the portal received more than 6,000 registrations in just two days. During a riff about global efforts to govern artificial intelligence, Trump said he was changing the technology’s name to “super intelligence” in all official U.S. documents. “The use of the word ‘artificial’ makes intelligence … sound fake, and it is not fake. It’s actually amazing,” he said. “In other words, welcome to the new world of super intelligence—SI,” he added. “It sounds much better, it is much better, and it’s more accurate. Let’s see if I have any power.” It just so happens that Slovenia, where U.S. first lady Melania Trump is from, owns and operates the “.si” domain name. The impact was immediate. More than 2,000 new websites were registered using the “.si” domain name on the day of Trump’s speech and more than 4,000 the day after, according to data shared by the Academic and Research Network of Slovenia (ARNES), which oversees the country’s domain registration portal. That brought the number of domain registrations in September to over 10,000 and pushed the overall number of web domains registered with the country past 200,000 for the first time ever. U.S. President Donald Trump made threats, while outgoing U.N. Secretary-General António Guterres called for peace and multilateralism. Brazil needs a China strategy adapted to all three changes. The election debate remains organized around competing preferences for Beijing and Washington, with too little attention to the economic and diplomatic capabilities Brasília needs to preserve its freedom of action. Lula’s approach has a coherent political logic. His electoral program prioritizes closer relations with the BRICS grouping and the global south, while he advocates greater use of national currencies in international transactions. * Analysis: Lula presidency saved at least 20,000km2 of Brazilian Amazon since 2022
  Carbon Brief | 28 Sep 10:35 UTC
  An area of Amazon forest roughly the size of the US state of New Jersey has remained standing due to… The post Analysis: Lula presidency saved at least 20,000km2 of Brazilian Amazon since 2022 appeared first on Carbon Brief .
  FULL TEXT: An area of Amazon forest roughly the size of the US state of New Jersey has remained standing due to Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva’s leadership of Brazil, according to Carbon Brief analysis. Lula beat Jair Bolsonaro in the 2022 election to become president of the nation that is home to nearly 60% of the Amazon rainforest. Forest loss surged during Bolsonaro’s far-right presidency and dropped sharply under the left-wing Lula, with Amazon deforestation likely to hit its lowest level on record this year. Now, as another presidential election approaches, Lula is facing off against Bolsonaro’s son, Flávio Bolsonaro, whose “ anti-environmental ” policies are similar to his father’s. Carbon Brief’s analysis suggests that at least 20,000 square kilometres (km2) of deforestation has been avoided since Lula took office and prioritised Amazon protection. The analysis is based on modelling by an international research team of an alternative scenario in which Brazil’s flagship forest code legislation was not enforced – a proxy for Jair Bolsonaro’s leadership. Experts tell Carbon Brief that this estimate is likely “conservative” and that actual deforestation under Bolsonaro could have been “much higher”. With Lula and Flávio Bolsonaro tied in the polls, the upcoming election is expected to significantly shape the level of environmental action in the world’s fourth-largest emitter, whose emissions are largely driven by deforestation. When Jair Bolsonaro was president of Brazil between 2019 and 2022, he championed the nation’s powerful agribusiness sector and oversaw an unprecedented increase in Amazon deforestation. Bolsonaro weakened regulations, slashed federal agency budgets and empowered illegal activities in the Amazon, while attacking Indigenous and environmental groups. This meant that the forest code – Brazil’s flagship legislation that requires landowners to preserve and restore forest on their property – was not properly enforced. After Lula’s presidential victory in 2022, he promised to target “ zero deforestation ” by 2030, telling the COP27 UN climate summit that “Brazil is back”. Lula rolled back Bolsonaro’s wave of deregulation and reinstated a deforestation “ action plan ”. Led by celebrated environmentalist Marina Silva, the environment ministry scaled up enforcement and policing activities in the Amazon. As a result, annual deforestation in the Amazon has more than halved from 11,594km2 per year in 2022 to 5,731km2 in 2025. Data from August 2025 to July 202
  https://www.carbonbrief.org/analysis-lula-presidency-saved-at-least-20000km2-of-brazilian-amazon-since-2022

* Months After the $292M Kelp Hack, Chainlink Lets Institutions Add Their Own Bridge Checks
  Decrypt | 28 Sep 17:51 UTC [also carried by: Google News Chainlink]
  Chainlink's CCIP 2.0 lets banks run their own security checks on cross-chain transfers, five months after a rival's setup lost $292 million to hackers.
  FULL TEXT: Chainlink today launched CCIP 2.0 , the newest version of its cross-chain plumbing—the software layer banks and crypto projects increasingly use to move tokenized money, like stablecoins, wrapped Bitcoin, and tokenized funds, between blockchains without building a bridge from scratch. That plumbing exists because blockchains don't talk to each other. Ethereum has no idea what's happening on Solana. So when a token moves from one chain to another, something has to confirm the money really left one place before it shows up on the other—that something is called a bridge, and it works by trusting a verifier to vouch for the transfer. That trust has been expensive. Bridges have lost billions to hackers over the years, usually because they lean on a single point of failure: one verifier, one thing to trick. CCIP 2.0's answer to that single-point-of-failure problem is a new feature called the Cross-Chain Verifier, or CCV. Institutions can now run their own verifier—a second guard checking the paperwork before a transfer clears—or hire one from a firm like Infosys or Nethermind. Starter kits are ready on Amazon Web Services and Google Cloud. Underneath, Chainlink still runs its default check: a committee of 16 independent node operators (16 separate companies that must all agree a transaction is legitimate) that reaches consensus on every transfer. That part hasn't changed. What has changed is quieter. The Risk Management Network—a separate set of nodes that used to double-check the main committee's work—is now less relevant. "The Risk Management Network's automated offchain role is no longer active in current CCIP deployments, but is expected to be offered as an optional validation layer in future releases," the documentation reads. The on-chain contract sticks around only as an emergency backstop. Chainlink says that same kind of independent check can come from the optional CCVs instead. In practice, that means an institution that adds nothing extra relies on one verification network, where it used to have two. This isn't just a DeFi trader's problem anymore. Chainlink says $15 billion in tokenized assets migrated onto its rails in the last four months, including chunks of BitGo's wrapped Bitcoin and Coinbase's cbBTC—assets increasingly sitting behind ETFs and bank products that regular people hold without ever touching a crypto wallet. The timing traces back to April, when hackers linked to North Korea's Lazarus Group drained about $292 million from Kelp DAO , 
  https://decrypt.co/379463/chainlink-institutions-add-bridge-checks-kelp-hack

* Oh, How Lucky We Are
  Longreads | 28 Sep 17:48 UTC
  "Sandy West believed that embracing what we cannot understand is the pathway to enlightenment, and that time on her beloved Ossabaw was the best way to learn that lesson."
  FULL TEXT: Beryl Gilothwest’s grandmother, Sandy “Moose” West, passed away at age 108 in 2021. For The Bitter Southerner , Gilothwest profiles a diminutive woman whose love for Ossabaw, an island off the coast of Georgia, was larger than life. In a world striving for “progress,” West was a staunch advocate for thoughtful preservation; she was a fierce conservationist and environmentalist who embraced the unknown and took pleasure in learning from it. “Sandy believed that embracing what we cannot understand is the pathway to enlightenment. In her mind, spending time in the uncharted wilds of Ossabaw is the best way to learn that lesson. Separated from the mainland by a 30-minute boat ride, the island is deeply remote. Cell service is scant, and there is no wi-fi. You have to fend for yourself out there in a way that is uncommon in contemporary culture, where we always seem to have an escape hatch. SK Hynix is deepening ties with the U.S. AI industry, including a $4 billion advanced packaging facility in Indiana, where it broke ground in August. Samsung, too, has been slowly and steadily getting its foot in the American door. * Reasoning together
  Aeon | 25 Sep 10:00 UTC
  Jürgen Habermas, the great defender of deliberative democracy, lived up to its demands: he never feared changing his mind - by Emilie Prattico Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: Jürgen Habermas at the philosophical faculty of Frankfurt University, Germany, in January 1969. All photos by Max Scheler/Süddeutsche Zeitung/Alamy Jürgen Habermas died on 14 March 2026, in the German town of Starnberg, aged 96. Every fall, from the mid-1990s into the late-2000s, he would come to Northwestern University in Evanston, Illinois to teach and give talks, a visit that a dedicated group of students looked forward to every fall term. The year that has stayed with me is 2004, when he taught what he’d spelled out in Between Facts and Norms (1992), his opus applying his theory to democratic politics. Law, philosophy, history, political science: students spanned many disciplines to come watch the famous German scholar every week, dressed in Wrangler jeans and a tweed jacket, expose his theory of deliberative politics in his unmistakable nasal voice, thick German accent, and impeccable English. Max Weber and the Frankfurt School, which were intimate to him, since he’d been Theodor Adorno’s research assistant in the 1950s, came to life in his speech and then in schemas and figures in chalk as he theorised about them. I understood almost none of it at first: words I’d read were coming to life in the room, spoken by their author, and still not resolving into sense. After several lectures, the shape came into focus: this wasn’t just Between Facts and Norms being rebuilt in public, but decades of work arriving at a single place. Since The Theory of Communicative Action (1981), Habermas had been arguing that reason survives in modern life only as something people do together, that is, by giving each other reasons and staying open to being moved by them, rather than by force, money or tradition. Between Facts and Norms was that idea turned toward law and democracy directly: a state counts as legitimate only when its laws could in principle be justified to everyone bound by them, through exactly that kind of open exchange. I’d watched that argument take root in a decades-long intellectual history. Thinking is a practice, it involves owing each other explanations, questions, answers. It is not a fast process, and doing it in public, out loud, in front of a room, is what makes it worthwhile. I attended all of Habermas’s seminars during my time at Northwestern with great interest and, to be honest, a sense of awe. One day, the topic of the weekly seminar was inclusion, and I had argued, with the timid conviction of a green graduate student, that deliberative publ

  "From a certain height, the problem becomes one of knowledge itself: Do you believe what you hear, or do you hear what you believe?"
  FULL TEXT: Audiophiles are sometimes described in terms reserved for cults; a 1956 New Yorker story, referenced in Colin Vanderburg’s exceptional tour of extreme listening, refers to “a remarkably articulate group of zealots” and “an almost mystical reverence for sound.” Vanderburg travels among the believers, tracing the decades-long promise of “consumer audio innovations” to their technological apex: the New York International Audio Show, where audiophiles sample six-figure sound systems in pursuit of a signal as pure as the world they already inhabit. More than anything, I have spent long passages of this earthly life sitting on my ass, listening. I listen to music, but what I hear is sound. Texture, timbre, and tone; clarity and detail; space and depth. And I hear, even more acutely, what thwarts sound. Shrill treble, muddled bass — for me these are worse than peeves. They offend hearing. They diminish the human sensorium. Earbuds repulse me; laptop speakers grate; a tabletop Bluetooth unit is a kind of vandalism. The word audiophile — etymologically, one who “loves to hear” — has a wine-sipping pretension that I hate, but sometimes an ugly shoe fits. Like similarly suffixed psychopathologies, the term suggests a state of warped dependence, of inbent sublimation. I didn’t set out to be this way, but neither have I ever sought help. My symptoms are apparent, their causes obscure. What, after all, do we love when we love sound? “The neurophysiological disorder is characterized by a severe aversion to sound—and the struggle to convince others of the severity of that aversion.” “How techno’s most vaunted architect is still building sonic futures.” “A physics expert and audio engineer may have solved the mystery of a strange noise that rattled an Oregon town in 2016.”
  https://longreads.com/2026/09/24/audiophile-new-york-international-audio-show/

  FULL TEXT: R.101 airship parked in Cardington, UK. Image by the Royal Aeronautical Society In this short video, Bill Hammack, an engineer and professor at the University of Illinois Urbana-Champaign, details the extraordinary making and tragic ending of the British airship known as R101. Conceived in 1924 and first flown in 1929, R101 was intended to fly a regular route from England to India. Hammack uses archival images and his own deep research to trace the engineering and history of this remarkable vessel, which the British government planned as the flagship of a fleet that would criss-cross its then-sprawling empire. Those ambitious plans were abandoned after the airship crashed in France in October 1930, killing 48 of the 54 people on board. Hammack offers a riveting peek into a brief moment in aviation history – one ended for good by the crash of the Hindenburg in 1937 – when it appeared as if commercial airships might become a regular sight in skies across the world. The video accompanies Hammack’s book Fatal Flight: The True Story of Britain’s Last Great Airship (2017), which is available as a free audiobook at his website . Photo by Randy Jackson Forty iron rectangles, each one blunt as a piano key, rotate into a blurring fury that hides the eight-foot-long shaft at the heart of the flail chopper. I am perched atop several tons of iron, with 80 horsepower at my command. This part of farming is straightforward – even a mechanically challenged tree-hugger like me can manage to depress the clutch, shift into second, and ease the throttle forward. The tractor’s tall, deeply treaded tires churn, revolving into a smooth spin. Everything is spinning: fans, belts, alternator, the driveshaft, axles. I don’t understand the mechanism fully, yet the tractor and chopper and I are propelled forward across the knee-high whiskers of a coarsely bearded cornfield. It is Indian summer in southern Wisconsin. My wife and I and our young son live here on her family’s farm. Both of us are employed off the farm, but on weekends and many evenings I am an apprentice to my father-in-law, David. Today, we are taking advantage of a brief period of warm, dry weather. Winter is approaching, and I could be spending the day beside our woodsplitter – the gasoline-sipping, hydraulically heroic mechanism that makes it possible to put up several cords of firewood quickly each year – but that work can wait until the cool, sunny days of fall are shoved aside by the relentless spin of the earth into darkness, sleet, snow and frozen ground. Managed grazing beef farm in the southern Wisconsin Driftless Area, Cavern Point Farm, Marshfield, Wisconsin. Photo by Randy Jackson The wall of tall green corn that stood here this summer has been transformed by fall and harvest. Little is standing after the field was stripped by a seed-combing combine. The mechanical tornado flails so loudly behind me: wailing against the air, grinding off the root-buttressed corn-stalk stumps, sucking up spent leaves and stripped cobs. In my wake, dry corn litter passes through the chopper like water compelled into frothy turbulence, falling again into an even, silent surface that hides so much activity below. No time to waste – the weather is expected to hold for another day, and I need to cover the field before the light fails this afternoon. The post Should Texas Schools Display the Ten Commandments? appeared first on New Lines Ma...
  FULL TEXT: America prides itself on religious freedom, a principle that atheist Nichole Manning passionately believes Texas refuses to extend to her family. Earlier this year, Texas passed a law requiring schools to prominently display posters of the Ten Commandments. (These posters were donated by private organizations, so U.S. tax dollars would not pay for them outright.) Manning joined Christian, Jewish, Hindu and other families in challenging the law as unconstitutional. The 5th U.S. Circuit Court of Appeals found the law constitutional in April, so it remains in effect. A petition to review the case is pending before the U.S. Supreme Court. There is a serious possibility that the court, with its conservative majority, will allow the law to stand. But if justices weigh history as heavily as they have in other major cases, the historical record actually gives the plaintiffs a strong argument of their own. Behind the constitutional dispute lies an older question. How should public schools handle religion? Americans have argued for centuries over what role, if any, religion should play in public education and whose religion public schools should emphasize. Whatever the Supreme Court eventually decides about the Ten Commandments, that debate will continue. Existing Supreme Court precedent about Ten Commandments displays is muddled. For many years, the Supreme Court relied on the framework from Lemon v. Kurtzman to decide whether a state had violated the First Amendment’s prohibition on laws “respecting an establishment of religion.” That framework famously focused on avoiding an “excessive government entanglement with religion.” Applying this test, the U.S. Supreme Court found it unconstitutional to use public money to pay for certain expenses at private religious schools, including supplements to teachers’ salaries. In 1980, the Supreme Court used the Lemon v. Kurtzman framework to evaluate a Kentucky law mandating that every public school classroom post the Ten Commandments. In a short opinion, the court concluded that the law violated the First Amendment’s Establishment Clause because the “pre-eminent purpose for posting the Ten Commandments on schoolroom walls is plainly religious in nature.” It acknowledged that public schools could include study of the Ten Commandments and other portions of the Bible as part of a curriculum to help students better understand history or literature. * Annie Dillard and the Writing Life
  Longreads | 22 Sep 15:41 UTC
  "Writers aren’t born, they’re made—from practice, reading, and a lot of caffeine. And sometimes tutelage."
  FULL TEXT: Count the verbs in Alexander Chee’s essay, note the absence of emotional language, and then thank Annie Dillard for her part. Chee, an essayist and fiction writer, revisits his undergraduate writing workshop with Dillard, the distinguished, Pulitzer Prize-winning nonfiction writer who died last week at 81. Chee’s account of their semester together is an affectionate portrait that doubles as a brisk craft talk; in it, he puts Dillard’s lessons through their paces, a fitting tribute to the teacher who borrowed cigarette drags from her students, chewed through caramel candies during class, and nurtured his own artistic evolution with her rules and exercises. Narrative writing sets down details in an order that evokes the writer’s experience for the reader, she announced. This seemed obvious but also radical—no one had ever said it so plainly to us. She spoke often of “the job.” If you’re doing your job, the reader feels what you felt. You don’t have to tell the reader how to feel. No one likes to be told how to feel about something. And if you doubt that, just go ahead. Try and tell someone how to feel. One afternoon, at her direction, we brought in our pages, scissors and tape, and told to bring several drafts of an essay, one that we struggled with over many versions. Now cut out only the best sentences, she said. And tape them on a blank page. And then when you have that, write in around them, she said. * Water Scarcity in Jordan Is Causing an Agricultural Crisis
  New Lines | 22 Sep 11:32 UTC
  In Jordan's Azraq oasis, farmers are struggling to survive as climate change and decades of groundwater depletion ruin their prospects. The post Water Scarcity in Jordan Is Causing an Agricultural Crisis appeared first on New Lines Magazine .
  FULL TEXT: In Jordan, people remember retreating shorelines. From the oasis in Azraq to the Dead Sea in the Jordan Valley, bodies of water have been shrinking, leaving behind cratered land and shriveled shrubs in their wake. When asked about the former glory of their country’s wetland areas, residents are gripped by a powerful wistfulness. In the town of Azraq, 67 miles from the capital city of Amman, everyone who was born before the 1990s has a story to tell about the oasis that once was. Naif Alafeer used to wrangle large fish with his friends, Fadi Esaid used to cup his hands in the water and drink from them after hours of playing in the sun, and Shaira Alafeer used to dig with her fingers just beneath the surface to access the water that lurked there. Azraq — which means blue in Arabic — was the perfect moniker for the freshwater pools and springs that lapped at one’s feet. In the early 20th century, when residents initially settled in the area, they bought arable land because the brimming streams promised them perpetually irrigated farms and bountiful harvests. But I saw a vast, unending landscape before me. The ground cracked underfoot, a reminder of why Jordan ranks among the world’s most water-poor nations. Azraq’s story is a microcosm of the larger tragedy unfolding in the Hashemite Kingdom of Jordan. Annual precipitation in the kingdom varies regionally between roughly 2 and 7.5 inches. Each person in Jordan is projected to have access to only 16,000 gallons of potable water annually. By comparison, 2024 data suggests that New York City’s 8.4 million residents had a per capita consumption of 118.19 gallons per person daily, which puts their annual consumption at roughly 43,139 gallons. Jordan relies on a public water system that supplies allotments of water once or twice a week to individual households. As a result, rooftop water tanks have become a permanent fixture of Jordan’s cityscapes. “A single distribution zone gets around 36 hours of water supply per week, but in the north, there are only six hours of water supply per week,” said Christian Klassert, a research associate who consults with Stanford University’s Jordan Water Project. While the country requires 1.4 billion cubic meters of water annually, it was already running a deficit of about 500 million cubic meters as of 2023.   https://newlinesmag.com/reportage/water-scarcity-in-jordan-is-causing-an-agricultural-crisis/

We stopped in Flint to say goodbye to family and retrofit the motorhome for the road. Mark’s father had died that June. His mother was devastated, and for a while the question wasn’t which route to choose but whether to go at all. We left Flint on October 4. Five miles later, the motorhome shut down. A few miles after the fuel line was fixed, we were surprised by a flat tire on the van’s tow dolly, and then, unbelievably, we bent a bumper while putting the van back on the dolly. It was past seven in the evening. We were finally on our way to Cleveland, Ohio. That was day one. What we built over the following nine months—25,000 miles through 40 states—would go on to earn a Yahoo! Cool Site designation, a presentation at a US Department of Education national conference, and a place in the federal Education Resources Information Center archive. On the road, we filed over 1,500 emails and website dispatches from museum lobbies, hotel reception desks, libraries, and wherever we could find a phone jack and someone willing to let us run a telephone cord to their wall. Google would not exist for another two years. The word “blog” had not yet been coined. We were doing something that had no name and no manual, and we were doing it over a dial-up modem while living in a motorhome. Blondins’ Assignment America was both the name of our trip and the website we built in real time as we traveled. We had a partnership with Northwest Academy, a science and technology charter school in northern Michigan in its first year of operation, that made it possible.   FULL TEXT: In the U.S., the “AI race” is understood as a distinctly anti-China battle, framing model performance as the deciding factor. U.S. policymakers have, across multiple administrations, shaped the country’s entire regulatory stance to be distinctly light-touch, underpinned by the belief that regulation could lose us this global battle for artificial intelligence dominance. Recently, OpenAI CEO Sam Altman admitted the AI revolution was going slower than expected, blaming “human habits and institutional inertia.” The data tells a different story: Americans increasingly distrust the technology even as AI companies race to place it at the center of everyday life. A majority of Americans are now more concerned than excited about AI, while trust in the government to regulate AI effectively remains weak . The problem is not confined to abstract anxiety about the future. China has recognized that governing AI means governing its deployment, not merely the capabilities of its models. AI is beginning to reshape electoral politics, becoming the hot-button issue for the U.S. midterm elections. Conservative voters, angry about the water, electricity, and land demands of AI infrastructure such as data centers, say they are preparing to “ break rank .” The technology industry has discovered a basic political truth: People will not support an AI future if they believe its costs are being imposed on them while its benefits accrue elsewhere. In stark contrast, what little conversation is entertained in Washington focuses on narratives of “uncontrollable rogue agents” and loss of control. Consumer protection is too often treated as the mundane cousin of AI safety. Meanwhile, lawsuits accumulate on harmful manipulation, AI toys marketed to children surface explicit content, commonly available AI models easily allow nonconsensual deepfakes, and AI introduced in online maps amplifies misinformation. Effective consumer protections give users agency: the ability to know when they are interacting with an AI system, understand when AI has generated or altered information, challenge consequential automated decisions, and seek redress when things go wrong. Absent these protections, younger “digital native” Americans are clear : They will not willingly purchase or use these products. There is an uncomfortable geopolitical irony here. * Toadstools and toxins
  Aeon | 22 Sep 10:00 UTC
  When I found the mushroom Amanita muscaria being sold in candy wrappers, I uncovered legal loopholes, dangerous dosages and more - by Eric Leas Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: Dried caps of the mushroom Amanita muscaria . Photo by Yarphoto/Getty Images In late summer 2023, I found myself in a smoke shop looking at a package of ‘magic mushroom’ gummies. That phrase, magic mushrooms, usually calls to mind one type of mushroom: those containing psilocybin, the compound showing promise as a therapy for depression, addiction and other psychopathologies. But these gummies were not psilocybin. Instead, the packaging described Amanita muscaria , the red-capped, white-spotted toadstool of fairy-tale forests, iPhone keyboards and Super Mario. I knew a little about the folklore of Amanita muscaria . I had first seen it discussed in an episode of the show Hamilton’s Pharmacopeia (2016-21), which begins with a claim I now hear often: that Amanita muscaria may be among the oldest human intoxicants. The New York banker-turned-ethnomycologist R Gordon Wasson, whose Life magazine essay from 1957 introduced psilocybin mushrooms to a mass Western audience, certainly took this idea seriously. In his book Soma: Divine Mushroom of Immortality (1968), he argued that the Vedic soma – a ritual drink in ancient Indian religions – was made from Amanita muscaria . The mushroom has also been linked, sometimes carefully and sometimes wildly, to the origins of Santa Claus , Viking berserkers and even Christianity. Some believe its other name, ‘fly agaric’, stems from its use as a natural pesticide for house flies; the word muscaria is also derived from the Latin word musca , meaning fly. Some scholars have linked Amanita muscaria to Lewis Carroll’s mushroom scene in Alice’s Adventures in Wonderland (1865), in which the Caterpillar tells Alice that ‘one side’ of the mushroom will make her taller and the other shorter, though Carroll does not identify a species. From the 1922 edition of Alice’s Adventures in Wonderland , illustrated by Gwynedd M Hudson. Photo by Alamy But I wasn’t standing in that smoke shop holding one of these products as a folklorist or looking to lose myself in Carroll’s ‘golden afternoon’. I am a public-health scientist, and my questions were pragmatic: what exactly was in this package? Could it hurt someone? Were these products legal? The first thing I did was what I usually do. I went to the scientific literature. And down the rabbit hole I tumbled.
